My husband and I are arguing more, over little things, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routine the way I used to. It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. If it’s one I’m expected to attend, I’ll find myself hanging back or hoping someone else covers it. Which is ridiculous, because I used to be pretty dependable about that. And internal deadlines—I haven’t exactly avoided them on purpose, but I put them off, and then suddenly they’re missed.\n\nIt’s like when my husband asks if I want to sit down after dinner and do our usual cleanup-and-settle-in thing, I just… drift away from it. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I mean, we’d bicker about normal things—he loads the dishwasher like he’s building a puzzle—but now it turns into more than it should.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’m avoiding meetings and some of the regular routines at home, even if I don’t always admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to fulfill your role at work or at home because of these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. We had this normal rhythm, just dinner and cleanup and settling down, nothing exciting, but it mattered. Now I get sidetracked or irritated, and then it turns into an argument. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. It’s more that most of what I’ve noticed has been work and home, because those are right in front of me every day. Friends are different, you know? You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Yes… I think it is. I mean, outside of work I’m still physically there, you know? I come home, I make dinner sometimes, or I start to, and then I get distracted thinking about something from the day.

Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. I mostly just get stuck thinking about stuff, and then I’ll maybe look over something again because I’m worried I missed something—but I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just me being… wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. And then at home, it’s like the smallest things turn into arguments with my husband. Even our normal after-dinner routine, which used to be just this ordinary little thing, I’m not really keeping up with it the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to have this whole pattern after dinner—nothing special, just dishes and settling in—and now even that feels harder than it should. So… yes, I’d say it’s interfering with daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is. I mean, I keep thinking I should be able to just handle it because it’s work, and everybody has someone above them criticizing things sometimes. But ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just feel wound up all the time.\n\nI’ll come home and I’m still in my head about it—like replaying meetings, thinking about what I should have said, or whether I missed something. And then I snap at my husband over something that isn’t even his fault. He’ll ask a normal question, like about dinner or the mail, and I’m already irritated because my mind is back at work. So yeah… it’s causing me a lot of distress. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve always been the kind of person who keeps track of things—my desk calendar, the little sticky notes, all of that. I used to tease my husband because he can never remember where he put his glasses, and now I’m the one letting things slip.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been showing up too. My husband and I are arguing more, over little things, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routine the way I used to. It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. We had this normal rhythm, just dinner and cleanup and settling down, nothing exciting, but it mattered. Now I get sidetracked or irritated, and then it turns into an argument. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. It’s more that most of what I’ve noticed has been work and home, because those are right in front of me every day. Friends are different, you know? You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. But I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed anything specific with friends. Not the way I have with my husband or at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are related to a specific fear or concern that you're trying to prevent or fix?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. It’s mostly about work. This new operations manager started, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Like I’m trying to figure out what I did wrong, or how to keep him from finding something else to criticize. Then I come home still wound up from it, and my husband asks some normal question and I snap at him like he’s the one doing it.\n\nSo yes, I guess the repetitive thoughts are tied to that fear that I’m going to mess up a quality call, or that something I’ve done routinely for years is suddenly not good enough. I used to make those calls without second-guessing every little thing. Now I can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation at work is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t feel like I’m participating in home life the way I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your current state is making it hard for you to enjoy time with your husband or engage in activities you both like?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My work record has taken a hit because I’ve missed internal deadlines and pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. At home, my husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I mean, we still sit in the same room and all that, but it doesn’t feel the same. We used to have our little evening routine—nothing fancy, dinner, cleaning up, maybe watching one of our shows. Sometimes he’d make popcorn, which is funny because he always burns it a little and says that’s how he likes it… anyway.

Lately I’m just not really in it. I come home already wound up, and then I don’t keep up with the usual routine the way I used to. Or he’ll ask something simple and I take it wrong, and then we’re arguing over things that shouldn’t be that big.

And it’s not just home, honestly—my work record has taken a hit too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to attend. Then I carry that home, and it makes the time with my husband harder to enjoy.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because work has been getting to me in a way it never used to. I’ve been an inspector for years, and I know my procedures, you know? But we got this new operations manager, and he keeps picking apart how I do inspections, questioning things I’ve done the same way forever.\n\nAnd I try to just leave it there, at work, but I can’t. I come home all wound up, and then my husband will say something normal, like about dinner or the TV being too loud, and I snap at him. Then at night I’m lying there replaying the meetings, what I should’ve said, what he meant by this or that. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nIt’s just… it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in these situations at work or when you're thinking about them later?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly, but I get really wound up. Like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to just leave it at work.\n\nI’ll come home still thinking about it, and then my husband says something completely normal and I snap at him, which isn’t fair. And then later I’m lying there replaying the meetings in my head—what he said, what I should’ve said. It’s like when I used to train new people, I’d always tell them, “Trust your process,” and now I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. So… yes, it definitely follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it. I get stuck on it. I’ll be home, and I’m supposed to be making dinner or watching something with my husband, and instead I’m back in that meeting in my head, hearing the operations manager pick apart how I did an inspection.\n\nAnd it’s silly because I’ve done this work for years — I know the procedures — but then I start replaying every little thing. What he meant, whether I should’ve said something different, whether I missed something. My husband will ask some normal question and I’ll snap because I’m still wound up from work. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It just keeps looping, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been pulling back some. Not like I’m refusing to work or anything, but I’ve missed a couple internal deadlines because I keep circling around things or putting off dealing with certain parts until I feel ready.\n\nAnd meetings… I’ve been avoiding some of those, or staying quiet when I’m expected to be there and speak up. Which is not really me. I used to be the one reminding everyone what had to get done.\n\nIt’s sort of spilled over at home too. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our normal evening stuff like I used to. We used to have our little routine after dinner, and lately I just… don’t have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop these repetitive thoughts, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes… I try to shut it off. I’ll tell myself, “You’re home now, leave it alone,” and then two minutes later I’m right back thinking about what that operations manager said about my inspection procedures.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ve done this for years. I used to trust my own calls, and now I’m lying awake replaying meetings and second-guessing routine quality decisions the next day. And then I’m wound up at home and snap at my husband over nothing, which makes me feel worse. So, yes, I’m trying to stop it, but it doesn’t really listen.\n\nDoctor: Are you also avoiding social situations or gatherings outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really been… going out much, but I don’t know if that’s avoiding social things or just being tired and preoccupied.\n\nLike, we were invited to a neighbor’s barbecue a while back—well, not a big thing, just one of those driveway things they do when the weather’s nice—and I remember thinking I should go because my husband likes chatting with them. But then I got caught up thinking about what I had to handle Monday, and then I didn’t really feel like making small talk. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little? But I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my husband. We’ve been arguing more than usual, and over things that normally wouldn’t turn into anything. Like dishes, or whether we’re doing our regular after-dinner routine… which sounds silly, but that routine used to kind of keep the evening normal.\n\nAnd I guess work relationships too, if that counts. My record there has taken a hit because I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. I used to be more on top of all that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about it as much. It’s more that things at home feel strained, and at work I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them irrational exactly. It’s not like I think something impossible is happening. The operations manager really is picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, so there’s a real thing there.\n\nBut the way I keep carrying it home with me… that part feels out of proportion, maybe. I’ll be standing in the kitchen trying to act normal, and my mind is back in some meeting, going over what I should’ve said. Then I get short with my husband, and later I’m lying there replaying it again. And the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nSo… I know it’s not helping. I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals, really. I mean, I do sometimes check things more carefully at work, but that’s also kind of the job, you know? We’re supposed to be careful.\n\nWhen you say rituals, I think of, like, having to touch a doorknob three times or line things up a certain way, and I don’t do anything like that. I mostly just get stuck thinking about stuff, and then I’ll maybe look over something again because I’m worried I missed something—but I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just me being… wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I keep telling myself I’m still getting through the day, so maybe it’s not that bad, but it’s definitely affecting things.\n\nAt work, my record’s taken a hit. I’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. And then at home, it’s like the smallest things turn into arguments with my husband. Even our normal after-dinner routine, which used to be just this ordinary little thing, I’m not really keeping up with it the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to have this whole pattern after dinner—nothing special, just dishes and settling in—and now even that feels harder than it should. So… yes, I’d say it’s interfering with daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is. I mean, I keep thinking I should be able to just handle it because it’s work, and everybody has someone above them criticizing things sometimes. But ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just feel wound up all the time.\n\nI’ll come home and I’m still in my head about it—like replaying meetings, thinking about what I should have said, or whether I missed something. And then I snap at my husband over something that isn’t even his fault. He’ll ask a normal question, like about dinner or the mail, and I’m already irritated because my mind is back at work. Then I lie awake feeling bad about that and go right back to thinking about work again.\n\nAnd the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So yeah… it’s causing me a lot of distress. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems with your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships due to these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself I’m still showing up, so it can’t be that serious, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be part of. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve always been the kind of person who keeps track of things—my desk calendar, the little sticky notes, all of that. I used to tease my husband because he can never remember where he put his glasses, and now I’m the one letting things slip.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been showing up too. My husband and I are arguing more, over little things, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routine the way I used to. It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. If it’s one I’m expected to attend, I’ll find myself hanging back or hoping someone else covers it. Which is ridiculous, because I used to be pretty dependable about that. And internal deadlines—I haven’t exactly avoided them on purpose, but I put them off, and then suddenly they’re missed.\n\nIt’s like when my husband asks if I want to sit down after dinner and do our usual cleanup-and-settle-in thing, I just… drift away from it. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I mean, we’d bicker about normal things—he loads the dishwasher like he’s building a puzzle—but now it turns into more than it should.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’m avoiding meetings and some of the regular routines at home, even if I don’t always admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to fulfill your role at work or at home because of these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. We had this normal rhythm, just dinner and cleanup and settling down, nothing exciting, but it mattered. Now I get sidetracked or irritated, and then it turns into an argument. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. It’s more that most of what I’ve noticed has been work and home, because those are right in front of me every day. Friends are different, you know? You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. But I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed anything specific with friends. Not the way I have with my husband or at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are related to a specific fear or concern that you're trying to prevent or fix?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. It’s mostly about work. This new operations manager started, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Like I’m trying to figure out what I did wrong, or how to keep him from finding something else to criticize. Then I come home still wound up from it, and my husband asks some normal question and I snap at him like he’s the one doing it.\n\nSo yes, I guess the repetitive thoughts are tied to that fear that I’m going to mess up a quality call, or that something I’ve done routinely for years is suddenly not good enough. I used to make those calls without second-guessing every little thing. Now I can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation at work is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, outside of work I’m still physically there, you know? I come home, I make dinner sometimes, or I start to, and then I get distracted thinking about something from the day.\n\nIt’s not like we had some glamorous routine—my husband and I just had our evenings, dinner, cleanup, maybe watching something. I used to keep up with that more. Now I’m irritated or still caught up in what happened at work, and then we argue over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before.\n\nAnd at work itself, it’s been showing too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m supposed to be in. Which then makes me feel worse, because I used to be pretty dependable with that stuff. So yes, it’s spilling over. I don’t feel like I’m participating in home life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is making it hard for you to enjoy time with your husband or engage in activities you both like?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, we still sit in the same room and all that, but it doesn’t feel the same. We used to have our little evening routine—nothing fancy, dinner, cleaning up, maybe watching one of our shows. Sometimes he’d make popcorn, which is funny because he always burns it a little and says that’s how he likes it… anyway.\n\nLately I’m just not really in it. I come home already wound up, and then I don’t keep up with the usual routine the way I used to. Or he’ll ask something simple and I take it wrong, and then we’re arguing over things that shouldn’t be that big.\n\nAnd it’s not just home, honestly—my work record has taken a hit too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to attend. Then I carry that home, and it makes the time with my husband harder to enjoy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F43.0", "F41.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. If it’s one I’m expected to attend, I’ll find myself hanging back or hoping someone else covers it. Which is ridiculous, because I used to be pretty dependable about that. And internal deadlines—I haven’t exactly avoided them on purpose, but I put them off, and then suddenly they’re missed.\n\nIt’s like when my husband asks if I want to sit down after dinner and do our usual cleanup-and-settle-in thing, I just… drift away from it. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I mean, we’d bicker about normal things—he loads the dishwasher like he’s building a puzzle—but now it turns into more than it should.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’m avoiding meetings and some of the regular routines at home, even if I don’t always admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to fulfill your role at work or at home because of these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. We had this normal rhythm, just dinner and cleanup and settling down, nothing exciting, but it mattered. Now I get sidetracked or irritated, and then it turns into an argument. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. It’s more that most of what I’ve noticed has been work and home, because those are right in front of me every day. Friends are different, you know? You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. But I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed anything specific with friends. Not the way I have with my husband or at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are related to a specific fear or concern that you're trying to prevent or fix?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. It’s mostly about work. This new operations manager started, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Like I’m trying to figure out what I did wrong, or how to keep him from finding something else to criticize. Then I come home still wound up from it, and my husband asks some normal question and I snap at him like he’s the one doing it.\n\nSo yes, I guess the repetitive thoughts are tied to that fear that I’m going to mess up a quality call, or that something I’ve done routinely for years is suddenly not good enough. I used to make those calls without second-guessing every little thing. Now I can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation at work is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, outside of work I’m still physically there, you know? I come home, I make dinner sometimes, or I start to, and then I get distracted thinking about something from the day.\n\nIt’s not like we had some glamorous routine—my husband and I just had our evenings, dinner, cleanup, maybe watching something. I used to keep up with that more. Now I’m irritated or still caught up in what happened at work, and then we argue over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before.\n\nAnd at work itself, it’s been showing too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m supposed to be in. Which then makes me feel worse, because I used to be pretty dependable with that stuff. So yes, it’s spilling over. Yes, I do. I don’t think I’m functioning the way I normally would.

At work, it’s showing up in things I used to be able to handle. My record has taken a hit—I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m expected to attend. I used to be pretty steady with that kind of thing, so that bothers me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because work has been getting to me in a way it never used to. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nIt’s just… it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in these situations at work or when you're thinking about them later?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly, but I get really wound up. Like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to just leave it at work.\n\nI’ll come home still thinking about it, and then my husband says something completely normal and I snap at him, which isn’t fair. And then later I’m lying there replaying the meetings in my head—what he said, what I should’ve said. So… yes, it definitely follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it. I get stuck on it. I’ll be home, and I’m supposed to be making dinner or watching something with my husband, and instead I’m back in that meeting in my head, hearing the operations manager pick apart how I did an inspection.\n\nAnd it’s silly because I’ve done this work for years — I know the procedures — but then I start replaying every little thing. What he meant, whether I should’ve said something different, whether I missed something. My husband will ask some normal question and I’ll snap because I’m still wound up from work. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It just keeps looping, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been pulling back some. Not like I’m refusing to work or anything, but I’ve missed a couple internal deadlines because I keep circling around things or putting off dealing with certain parts until I feel ready.\n\nAnd meetings… I’ve been avoiding some of those, or staying quiet when I’m expected to be there and speak up. Which is not really me. I used to be the one reminding everyone what had to get done.\n\nIt’s sort of spilled over at home too. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our normal evening stuff like I used to. We used to have our little routine after dinner, and lately I just… don’t have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop these repetitive thoughts, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes… I try to shut it off. I’ll tell myself, “You’re home now, leave it alone,” and then two minutes later I’m right back thinking about what that operations manager said about my inspection procedures.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ve done this for years. I used to trust my own calls, and now I’m lying awake replaying meetings and second-guessing routine quality decisions the next day. And then I’m wound up at home and snap at my husband over nothing, which makes me feel worse. So, yes, I’m trying to stop it, but it doesn’t really listen.\n\nDoctor: Are you also avoiding social situations or gatherings outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really been… going out much, but I don’t know if that’s avoiding social things or just being tired and preoccupied.\n\nLike, we were invited to a neighbor’s barbecue a while back—well, not a big thing, just one of those driveway things they do when the weather’s nice—and I remember thinking I should go because my husband likes chatting with them. But then I got caught up thinking about what I had to handle Monday, and then I didn’t really feel like making small talk. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little? But I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my husband. We’ve been arguing more than usual, and over things that normally wouldn’t turn into anything. Like dishes, or whether we’re doing our regular after-dinner routine… which sounds silly, but that routine used to kind of keep the evening normal.\n\nAnd I guess work relationships too, if that counts. My record there has taken a hit because I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. I used to be more on top of all that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about it as much. It’s more that things at home feel strained, and at work I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them irrational exactly. It’s not like I think something impossible is happening. The operations manager really is picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, so there’s a real thing there.\n\nBut the way I keep carrying it home with me… that part feels out of proportion, maybe. I’ll be standing in the kitchen trying to act normal, and my mind is back in some meeting, going over what I should’ve said. Then I get short with my husband, and later I’m lying there replaying it again. And the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nSo… I know it’s not helping. I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals, really. I mean, I do sometimes check things more carefully at work, but that’s also kind of the job, you know? We’re supposed to be careful.\n\nWhen you say rituals, I think of, like, having to touch a doorknob three times or line things up a certain way, and I don’t do anything like that. I mostly just get stuck thinking about stuff, and then I’ll maybe look over something again because I’m worried I missed something—but I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just me being… wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I keep telling myself I’m still getting through the day, so maybe it’s not that bad, but it’s definitely affecting things.\n\nAt work, my record’s taken a hit. I’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. And then at home, it’s like the smallest things turn into arguments with my husband. Even our normal after-dinner routine, which used to be just this ordinary little thing, I’m not really keeping up with it the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to have this whole pattern after dinner—nothing special, just dishes and settling in—and now even that feels harder than it should. So… yes, I’d say it’s interfering with daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is. I mean, I keep thinking I should be able to just handle it because it’s work, and everybody has someone above them criticizing things sometimes. But ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just feel wound up all the time.\n\nI’ll come home and I’m still in my head about it—like replaying meetings, thinking about what I should have said, or whether I missed something. And then I snap at my husband over something that isn’t even his fault. He’ll ask a normal question, like about dinner or the mail, and I’m already irritated because my mind is back at work. Then I lie awake feeling bad about that and go right back to thinking about work again.\n\nAnd the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So yeah… it’s causing me a lot of distress. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems with your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships due to these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself I’m still showing up, so it can’t be that serious, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be part of. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve always been the kind of person who keeps track of things—my desk calendar, the little sticky notes, all of that. I used to tease my husband because he can never remember where he put his glasses, and now I’m the one letting things slip.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been showing up too. My husband and I are arguing more, over little things, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routine the way I used to. It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. If it’s one I’m expected to attend, I’ll find myself hanging back or hoping someone else covers it. Which is ridiculous, because I used to be pretty dependable about that. And internal deadlines—I haven’t exactly avoided them on purpose, but I put them off, and then suddenly they’re missed.\n\nIt’s like when my husband asks if I want to sit down after dinner and do our usual cleanup-and-settle-in thing, I just… drift away from it. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I mean, we’d bicker about normal things—he loads the dishwasher like he’s building a puzzle—but now it turns into more than it should.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’m avoiding meetings and some of the regular routines at home, even if I don’t always admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to fulfill your role at work or at home because of these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. We had this normal rhythm, just dinner and cleanup and settling down, nothing exciting, but it mattered. Now I get sidetracked or irritated, and then it turns into an argument. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. It’s more that most of what I’ve noticed has been work and home, because those are right in front of me every day. Friends are different, you know? You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. But I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed anything specific with friends. Not the way I have with my husband or at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are related to a specific fear or concern that you're trying to prevent or fix?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. It’s mostly about work. This new operations manager started, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Like I’m trying to figure out what I did wrong, or how to keep him from finding something else to criticize. Then I come home still wound up from it, and my husband asks some normal question and I snap at him like he’s the one doing it.\n\nSo yes, I guess the repetitive thoughts are tied to that fear that I’m going to mess up a quality call, or that something I’ve done routinely for years is suddenly not good enough. I used to make those calls without second-guessing every little thing. Now I can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation at work is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, outside of work I’m still physically there, you know? I come home, I make dinner sometimes, or I start to, and then I get distracted thinking about something from the day.\n\nIt’s not like we had some glamorous routine—my husband and I just had our evenings, dinner, cleanup, maybe watching something. I used to keep up with that more. Now I’m irritated or still caught up in what happened at work, and then we argue over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before.\n\nAnd at work itself, it’s been showing too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m supposed to be in. Which then makes me feel worse, because I used to be pretty dependable with that stuff. So yes, it’s spilling over. I don’t feel like I’m participating in home life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is making it hard for you to enjoy time with your husband or engage in activities you both like?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, we still sit in the same room and all that, but it doesn’t feel the same. We used to have our little evening routine—nothing fancy, dinner, cleaning up, maybe watching one of our shows. Sometimes he’d make popcorn, which is funny because he always burns it a little and says that’s how he likes it… anyway.\n\nLately I’m just not really in it. I come home already wound up, and then I don’t keep up with the usual routine the way I used to. Or he’ll ask something simple and I take it wrong, and then we’re arguing over things that shouldn’t be that big.\n\nAnd it’s not just home, honestly—my work record has taken a hit too. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to attend. Then I carry that home, and it makes the time with my husband harder to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current situation is affecting your ability to function normally in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think I’m functioning the way I normally would.\n\nAt work, it’s showing up in things I used to be able to handle. My record has taken a hit—I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m expected to attend. I used to be pretty steady with that kind of thing, so that bothers me.\n\nAnd then at home… it’s not exactly falling apart, but my husband and I are arguing more. Sometimes it starts over something small, like dinner or what we’re doing that evening, and then suddenly it’s bigger than it should be. We had our usual routine after work, and I’m just not keeping up with it like I used to. I’ll mean to, and then I’m distracted or irritated, and the evening kind of gets away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes, unfortunately. I mean, I keep thinking I can just sort of push through it, because that’s what I’ve always done, but it’s not really working that way.

I come home all wound up, and then my husband will say something normal, like about dinner or the TV being too loud, and I snap at him. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nIt’s just… it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in these situations at work or when you're thinking about them later?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly, but I get really wound up. Like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to just leave it at work.\n\nI’ll come home still thinking about it, and then my husband says something completely normal and I snap at him, which isn’t fair. And then later I’m lying there replaying the meetings in my head—what he said, what I should’ve said. It’s like when I used to train new people, I’d always tell them, “Trust your process,” and now I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. So… yes, it definitely follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it. I get stuck on it. I’ll be home, and I’m supposed to be making dinner or watching something with my husband, and instead I’m back in that meeting in my head, hearing the operations manager pick apart how I did an inspection.\n\nAnd it’s silly because I’ve done this work for years — I know the procedures — but then I start replaying every little thing. What he meant, whether I should’ve said something different, whether I missed something. My husband will ask some normal question and I’ll snap because I’m still wound up from work. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It just keeps looping, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been pulling back some. Not like I’m refusing to work or anything, but I’ve missed a couple internal deadlines because I keep circling around things or putting off dealing with certain parts until I feel ready.\n\nAnd meetings… I’ve been avoiding some of those, or staying quiet when I’m expected to be there and speak up. Which is not really me. I used to be the one reminding everyone what had to get done.\n\nIt’s sort of spilled over at home too. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our normal evening stuff like I used to. We used to have our little routine after dinner, and lately I just… don’t have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop these repetitive thoughts, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes… I try to shut it off. I’ll tell myself, “You’re home now, leave it alone,” and then two minutes later I’m right back thinking about what that operations manager said about my inspection procedures.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ve done this for years. I used to trust my own calls, and now I’m lying awake replaying meetings and second-guessing routine quality decisions the next day. And then I’m wound up at home and snap at my husband over nothing, which makes me feel worse. So, yes, I’m trying to stop it, but it doesn’t really listen.\n\nDoctor: Are you also avoiding social situations or gatherings outside of work and home?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really been… going out much, but I don’t know if that’s avoiding social things or just being tired and preoccupied.\n\nLike, we were invited to a neighbor’s barbecue a while back—well, not a big thing, just one of those driveway things they do when the weather’s nice—and I remember thinking I should go because my husband likes chatting with them. But then I got caught up thinking about what I had to handle Monday, and then I didn’t really feel like making small talk. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little? But I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my husband. We’ve been arguing more than usual, and over things that normally wouldn’t turn into anything. Like dishes, or whether we’re doing our regular after-dinner routine… which sounds silly, but that routine used to kind of keep the evening normal.\n\nAnd I guess work relationships too, if that counts. My record there has taken a hit because I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. I used to be more on top of all that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about it as much. It’s more that things at home feel strained, and at work I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are irrational or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them irrational exactly. It’s not like I think something impossible is happening. The operations manager really is picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, so there’s a real thing there.\n\nBut the way I keep carrying it home with me… that part feels out of proportion, maybe. I’ll be standing in the kitchen trying to act normal, and my mind is back in some meeting, going over what I should’ve said. Then I get short with my husband, and later I’m lying there replaying it again. And the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I used to make without even thinking twice.\n\nSo… I know it’s not helping. I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals, really. I mean, I do sometimes check things more carefully at work, but that’s also kind of the job, you know? We’re supposed to be careful.\n\nWhen you say rituals, I think of, like, having to touch a doorknob three times or line things up a certain way, and I don’t do anything like that. I mostly just get stuck thinking about stuff, and then I’ll maybe look over something again because I’m worried I missed something—but I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just me being… wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I keep telling myself I’m still getting through the day, so maybe it’s not that bad, but it’s definitely affecting things.\n\nAt work, my record’s taken a hit. I’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in. And then at home, it’s like the smallest things turn into arguments with my husband. Even our normal after-dinner routine, which used to be just this ordinary little thing, I’m not really keeping up with it the same way.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to have this whole pattern after dinner—nothing special, just dishes and settling in—and now even that feels harder than it should. So… yes, I’d say it’s interfering with daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is. I mean, I keep thinking I should be able to just handle it because it’s work, and everybody has someone above them criticizing things sometimes. But ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just feel wound up all the time.\n\nI’ll come home and I’m still in my head about it—like replaying meetings, thinking about what I should have said, or whether I missed something. And then I snap at my husband over something that isn’t even his fault. He’ll ask a normal question, like about dinner or the mail, and I’m already irritated because my mind is back at work. Then I lie awake feeling bad about that and go right back to thinking about work again.\n\nAnd the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So yeah… it’s causing me a lot of distress. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems with your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships due to these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. I mean, I keep trying to tell myself I’m still showing up, so it can’t be that serious, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m supposed to be part of. Which is embarrassing, because I’ve always been the kind of person who keeps track of things—my desk calendar, the little sticky notes, all of that. I used to tease my husband because he can never remember where he put his glasses, and now I’m the one letting things slip.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been showing up too. My husband and I are arguing more, over little things, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routine the way I used to. It sounds small, but that routine was kind of how we stayed settled, I guess. So… yes, it’s affecting work and home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding altogether because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding everything altogether, but… yes, certain things I kind of steer around now.\n\nAt work, meetings especially. If it’s one I’m expected to attend, I’ll find myself hanging back or hoping someone else covers it. Which is ridiculous, because I used to be pretty dependable about that. And internal deadlines—I haven’t exactly avoided them on purpose, but I put them off, and then suddenly they’re missed.\n\nIt’s like when my husband asks if I want to sit down after dinner and do our usual cleanup-and-settle-in thing, I just… drift away from it. Then we end up arguing over something small. We never used to be like that as much. I mean, we’d bicker about normal things—he loads the dishwasher like he’s building a puzzle—but now it turns into more than it should.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’m avoiding meetings and some of the regular routines at home, even if I don’t always admit that’s what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to fulfill your role at work or at home because of these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying “unable,” because I’m still going in, I’m still trying to do the job, but I’m not doing it the way I used to.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve pulled back from meetings I’m expected to attend. And that’s not really like me. I used to be the one with everything written down, color-coded sometimes—well, not always color-coded, that makes me sound more organized than I am, but I had my system.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My husband and I are arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. So yes… I guess I do feel like I’m not fulfilling those roles the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with friends have been affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Friends… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some big falling-out story or anything. You can sort of go a while without talking and then tell yourself everyone’s just busy.\n\nI did see a neighbor the other day—well, she’s not exactly a friend, more like someone I chat with by the mailboxes—and she asked how I was, and I gave the usual “fine” answer. But I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed anything specific with friends. Not the way I have with my husband or at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts are related to a specific fear or concern that you're trying to prevent or fix?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. It’s mostly about work. This new operations manager started, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know procedures can change, I’m not saying everything I do is perfect—my old supervisor used to say I was thorough, sometimes too thorough, which… anyway.\n\nBut since he started questioning everything, I keep replaying it in my head. Like I’m trying to figure out what I did wrong, or how to keep him from finding something else to criticize. I’ve missed internal deadlines, and I’ve stopped going to some meetings I’m supposed to be in. Which then makes me feel worse, because I used to be pretty dependable with that stuff. So yes, it’s spilling over. I don’t feel like I’m participating in home life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current state is making it hard for you to enjoy time with your husband or engage in activities you both like?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. Sometimes he’d make popcorn, which is funny because he always burns it a little and says that’s how he likes it… anyway.\n\nLately I’m just not really in it. I come home already wound up, and then I don’t keep up with the usual routine the way I used to. Or he’ll ask something simple and I take it wrong, and then we’re arguing over things that shouldn’t be that big.\n\nAnd it’s not just home, honestly—my work record has taken a hit too. I used to be pretty steady with that kind of thing, so that bothers me.\n\nAnd then at home… it’s not exactly falling apart, but my husband and I are arguing more.
